So the stones work should treat "how wide is a path, in island-radii" as a **design input to decide**, not an output to inherit — probably by choosing stone size from stride first, then picking `STONE_COLS` to hit a target width fraction.
- **Also relevant**: `STONE_SIZE` is deliberately `1.0 × FIGURE_SCALE` — stones are sized relative to the *character*, not the world (see the scale-rework entry; Luke corrected an earlier pass that had this backwards). Real stone meshes must keep that relationship: they are things a person steps on, and their size is a stride question first and a scenery question second.
- **One deferred behaviour needs per-stone control**, which instanced billboards make awkward and real meshes may make easier: the wrong branch's stones should **crumble and drop away** as the player approaches, revealing the gap (currently they are simply absent from the moment the fork is built). Worth checking whether the stone representation chosen here makes that cheap.

### Path rework: floating islands at intersections
- **Status**: ✓ Superseded 2026-08-27 — the islands exist and ship. See "Fork islands: procedural 3D rock" and "Hand-modelled island" in Completed for what was actually built, and "World scale rework" in Pending for the spacing problem their arrival exposed. Kept for the original reasoning below, which still holds.
- ~~**Status**: Pending — needs visual design~~
- **What**: Each fork intersection will become a floating island in the sky rather than a section of the continuous path. This provides dedicated space for the team to gather (solving the crowding problem from the plaza prototype) without widening the main path itself.
- **Design TBD**: Visual appearance/image for the islands — need to model/render and add to the scene.
- **Why**: The current platform widening (plaza) was a one-off prototype hack. Floating islands are the architectural solution: each intersection becomes a distinct visual beat in the journey, islands provide room for multiple avatars without making the path itself unwieldy, and the design reads as a natural stopping point before committing to the next decision.
- **Files to update**: `src/main.js` — `buildFork()` will need to build an island mesh at the fork location instead of (or in addition to?) the plaza's scattered stones.
- **Dependency**: Visual asset (island texture/model) must be created first.

### Full integration: from five prototypes to one app (roadmap, agreed 2026-08-23)

- **Status**: Roadmap agreed. **Stage A1 (scaffold), A2 (extract the game) and A3 (port the lobby) are done and verified end to end across two real browser sessions and the real Supabase project.** Target is a **demo round**: students join a lobby, get assigned to a team, one is the guide, the team plays a real round of Sky Path together, and it can be played again with a different guide. That demo round now works.

#### The diagnosis: nobody owns a round

All five build-sequence steps have a working proof, but they are five separate static pages joined by a query string. The single line that defines the current ceiling is in [lobby-prototype/lobby.js](lobby-prototype/lobby.js):

```js
const url = `${SKY_PATH_BASE_URL}?forks=${forks}&role=${myRole}&room=${roomGroupId}`;
```

Sky Path is launched as a **new tab, stamped with its state at birth, that never talks back**. Every "known gap, deliberately deferred" recorded elsewhere in this file is a symptom of that one fact, not an independent bug:

| Deferred gap | Why it's actually the same problem |
|---|---|
| Guide can't rotate between rounds | Role is baked into a URL that has already been opened |
| "Again"/reset isn't networked | The game tab has no concept of a round ending for the room |
| Score never reaches the roster | The game tab has no identity store, no class id, no token |
| Guide disconnect unhandled | Nothing owns the round's lifecycle to notice or recover |

Fixing these four separately would be four hacks. They collapse into one fix: **make a round a real, owned, networked object**, which requires Sky Path to be a *component inside* the session rather than a *page launched from* it.

#### The target shape

One Vite app, one Supabase client, one session context. Sky Path stops reading `location.search` and stops importing Supabase — it goes back to being ignorant of the network, which is what makes it testable and what lets a second game mode (quiz) slot in beside it later.

```
SessionProvider   — device token, roster-backed identity, session presence channel
  └─ RoomProvider — room channel, round state machine, guide assignment
       └─ <SkyPath forks role onForkChoice onRoundEnd />   (options in, callbacks out)
```

#### Stages

- **Stage A — Consolidate. No new features.** Single Vite project; React shell; one config via `import.meta.env` instead of three separate gitignored `config.js` copies. Extract [src/main.js](prototype-threejs/src/main.js) — 2,194 lines of module-scope script reading 222 lines of hand-written DOM by ID — into a `mount(container, opts) → { dispose() }` module. The Three.js canvas stays imperative; the surrounding chrome (buttons, hint text, character carousel) becomes React. **Acceptance: solo Sky Path plays identically to today, and the lobby prototype's behaviour is reproduced inside the new app.** Deliberately zero new capability — that is the point, and it is why this stage is the one most likely to be skipped and most costly to skip.
- **Stage B — Real sessions and join flow.** A `sessions` table (join code → class id → teacher id). Teacher signs in, picks a class, starts a session, gets a code + QR. Student opens `/join/ABC123`, resolves the class, picks their name from the roster via the already-proven [supabaseIdentityStore.js](app/src/identity/supabaseIdentityStore.js), and falls back to the ephemeral local store if they aren't on the roster (the fallback the step-5 notes flag as designed-but-unwired). Presence channel becomes `session-<id>` rather than the hardcoded `lobby-room` — which also fixes the real bug observed during step-3 testing, where unrelated participants ("Bob", "Jill", "Jim") from outside the session appeared in the lobby, because every device everywhere shares one channel name.
- **Stage C — Teacher gating. ✓ Done and verified.** Group management renders only for the authed teacher client. `App.jsx` now tracks the Supabase auth session (same `getSession()`/`onAuthStateChange()` pattern `TeacherDashboard.jsx` already used) and passes `isTeacher` down through `Lobby.jsx`, which only mounts `<GroupEditor>` when it's true. Verified in the browser both directions: signed out, "Group management" is absent entirely from the lobby; signed in (even via a session that outlived a round-trip through the teacher dashboard and back), it reappears. The clobbering bug this stage was also meant to fix turned out not to exist in the React port — `GroupEditor`'s draft is real component state keyed by token, not derived from `participants` on every render, so it was never actually vulnerable the way the vanilla-JS prototype was. Verified directly: typed a draft group number for one participant, had a second device join then leave (two separate presence syncs), and the typed value survived both untouched.
- **Stage D — Round lifecycle. ✓ Done and verified.** *The actual prize, and the demo round.* An explicit state machine now lives in `useLobby.js`: `lobby → assigning → playing → results → lobby`. `game-started` (now carrying a `roundId`) and a new `round-ended` broadcast are both room-channel events, so the whole room transitions together rather than each device guessing from its own Sky Path instance's timing — `round-ended` uses the same "first message for this round wins" rule the fork-choice arbiter already used, so a late/duplicate broadcast can't reopen results. `GameRoom.jsx`'s `onRoundEnd` is now `reportRoundEnd` (was a `console.log` stub) — it broadcasts the result instead of just proving the seam exists. A new `RoundResults.jsx` screen shows the outcome, who guided, and "Play again"/"Back to lobby". Guide rotation is round-robin: `guideHistoryRef` in `useLobby.js` is rebuilt purely by replaying `game-started` broadcasts already received (never written to directly), so every device in a room computes the identical "who hasn't guided yet" set independent of who clicks the button — same decentralized pattern as fork-choice arbitration, no host needed. Once everyone in the group has guided once, the cycle resets.
  - **Verified with two real devices in one group, using the actual Sky Path app.** DeviceA and DeviceB joined room-1; DeviceA's "Start Sky Path" assigned it guide, DeviceB player (`window.__sections()` used to read the correct side per fork without needing the guide's own view). A correct pick on fork 1 advanced both devices from fork 1 to fork 2 in lockstep, confirming the round-lifecycle refactor didn't regress the already-proven fork-choice relay. A deliberate wrong pick on fork 2 produced the fall on both devices, and **both independently landed on the results screen with matching output** — "Fell at fork 3 of 6, after 1 safe crossing," DeviceA correctly labelled "(you) — you were the guide" on its own screen and "DeviceA — you were a player" on DeviceB's. Clicking "Play again" on DeviceA started a second round with **guide rotated to DeviceB** (confirmed on both screens: DeviceB now guide, DeviceA now player) — two rounds back to back, different guide each time, no URL touched, exactly the stage's acceptance bar.
- **Stage E — Score flows home.** Deferred pending decision on score-persistence model. Round end already emits a `result` payload up through the relay (the "points payload" that build-sequence step 3 called for), and `reportRoundEnd` in `useLobby.js` broadcasts it. What's missing is the logic to unpack that result and call `identityStore.addScore()` / `addEquipment()`. That step is straightforward plumbing once the score model is decided (e.g. fixed points per round vs. variable based on fork count vs. something else). **Acceptance: finish a round, see the updated score on the teacher's roster view, have it survive a page reload and a simulated new day.**
- **Stage F — Deploy and harden.** GitHub Pages build, QR generation, load-time budget measured on real phones, guide-disconnect handling, and the free-tier Supabase pause-after-a-week caveat.

#### Stage A progress

**A1 — scaffold. ✓ Done.** New [app/](app) — one Vite project (React 19, three, Rapier, `@supabase/supabase-js` as a real npm dependency rather than an esm.sh URL). Credentials move from four hand-copied gitignored `config.js` files to one `.env.local` (`VITE_SUPABASE_URL` / `VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY`, see `app/.env.example`), read by one shared client in [app/src/supabase.js](app/src/supabase.js). The identity modules were copied to `app/src/identity/` and their contract test still passes there unchanged (`npm test` in `app/`). Build target raised es2020 → **es2022**, because the game keeps its module-scope `await RAPIER.init()` and top-level await isn't valid below es2022. Dev server on **5181** while the old prototype still holds 5180, so the two can be compared side by side.

**A2 — extract the game. ✓ Done and verified.** [app/src/skypath/skyPath.js](app/src/skypath/skyPath.js) is `main.js` turned into `mountSkyPath(container, options) → handle`. The transform was done by script (indent + wrap) rather than by hand so the re-indentation is mechanical and the real edits are only the seams. Those seams, in full:

  | Was (owned the page) | Now (owns a panel) |
  |---|---|
  | `document.body.appendChild(canvas)` | `container.appendChild(canvas)` |
  | `window.innerWidth/Height` | `container.clientWidth/Height` |
  | `addEventListener('resize')` | `ResizeObserver`, disconnected on dispose |
  | `new URLSearchParams(location.search).get('forks')` | `options.forks` |
  | `initMultiplayer()` + Supabase import | `options.onForkChoice` / `handle.applyChoice()` |
  | `document.getElementById(...)` × 12 | `container.querySelector(...)` |
  | markup + CSS in `index.html` | [app/src/skypath/chrome.js](app/src/skypath/chrome.js), `position: fixed` → `absolute`, scoped under `.skypath-surface` |
  | (nothing) | `handle.dispose()` — cancels the loop, disconnects the observer, releases the WebGL context, clears the container |
  | (nothing) | `options.onRoundEnd({success, forkIndex, correctCount, totalForks})` |

  **A tap is now a request, not a decision.** `requestChoice()` calls `onForkChoice` and changes no state; the owner calls `handle.applyChoice(forkIndex, side)` back. Solo play (no `onForkChoice` supplied) settles it immediately. This is the shape the "a player taps" decision below needs — applying locally *as well* is the one thing that would reintroduce divergence.

  **Boundary drawn deliberately**: the game module owns its canvas *and* its in-game HUD, injecting that markup itself; React owns only what's outside the game surface. [app/src/skypath/SkyPath.jsx](app/src/skypath/SkyPath.jsx) is 50 lines — a sized div, a mount, a dispose — and holds its callbacks in a ref so a parent re-render can't tear down a round in progress. Routing a 60fps HUD through React would buy nothing.

- **Verified** (numerically, via pumped frames — see the testing note below): solo walk to fork 1 with buttons and guide markers appearing; a correct pick advancing fork 1 → 2 with `correctCount` and time-of-day both incrementing; a wrong pick falling under real physics (`y` → −135) with the card hiding at 5s, `finished` flipping at 2.2s while `falling` stays true, and the two-phase timing intact; the "Again" reset returning rotation to exactly `[0,0,0]` with the figure visible; a full second round playing after that reset; `?forks=RRLLRL` honoured (fork 1's correct side = `right`); the **anti-cheat invariant preserved** (only 1 section exists while fork 1 is undecided); player role hiding both markers and the answer in the hint; and `onRoundEnd` firing exactly once with `{success: false, forkIndex: 2, correctCount: 1, totalForks: 6}` — **the first time the game has ever reported a result upward**, which is the seam Stage E needs. A rendered frame was captured and inspected by eye (temple, character, pillars, correct green/red markers for `forks=LLLLLL`). Production build succeeds.
- **Bug found and fixed by the extraction — a dispose race.** Texture loads stay in flight for ~a second after mount, and `manager.onLoad` / `onProgress` fire whether or not the round still exists. Unmount inside that window (a teacher ending a round early; React StrictMode's mount/unmount/mount in dev) and `onLoad` ran against a container `dispose()` had already emptied, throwing `Cannot read properties of null (reading 'classList')` on `#loader`. Both callbacks now return early if `disposed`, as does the deferred `charSelect.classList.add('visible')`. Confirmed fixed by churning 12 mount-then-immediately-dispose cycles with a live `window.onerror` listener attached: **0 errors**, where the same churn previously produced one per cycle. This class of bug did not and could not exist while the game owned the whole page — it is exactly what Stage A was for.
- **Leak check, because Stage D remounts every round**: 11 consecutive mount/dispose cycles left the container empty, exhausted no WebGL contexts (browsers cap live contexts at ~8–16, so leaking one per round would break a lesson after a handful of rounds), and a fresh mount afterwards still worked with an unlost context.

- **Testing note — `requestAnimationFrame` is dead in the preview pane.** Reconfirmed this session: `document.hidden === true` and `document.visibilityState === 'hidden'` even with the pane fronted, so **no rAF callback ever fires** and `computer{action:"screenshot"}` times out because nothing composites. This first presented as two apparent bugs in the extraction — a frozen game loop, and the character-select overlay stuck at `display:flex; opacity:0` because its fade-in is scheduled via rAF — and neither was real. Verify by pumping `window.__tick()` in a loop with real `setTimeout` delays (so `THREE.Timer`'s deltas are genuine), and capture frames with `window.__capture()` POSTed to `/__shot`, which renders on demand and doesn't need compositing. The `shotPlugin` from the prototype's Vite config was carried into the app's for exactly this reason.

- **Flagged for Stage F, measured now so it isn't a surprise**: the production bundle is **3.6 MB raw / 1.30 MB gzipped** in a single chunk. The handoff makes load time and download size first-class requirements ("a slow first paint at the start of a lesson is the same failure mode that ruled out Render's sleeping free tier"), and this is a class of ~30 phones on school wifi. Most of it is Rapier's WASM (inlined as base64 by `rapier3d-compat`) plus Three. Rapier is only needed for the wrong-turn fall, so the obvious first move is a dynamic `import()` at `startFall()` rather than a top-level one — which also removes the module-scope top-level await and lets the target drop back to es2020. Not urgent, but do not let this drift upward unmeasured.

#### A3 — port the lobby. ✓ Done and verified against the real Supabase project.

[app/src/lobby/](app/src/lobby) ports lobby-prototype/lobby.js into React. The data model and Supabase wiring are **unchanged** from the standalone prototype — presence keyed by identity token, grouping as a broadcast `{token: groupId}` map applied locally by each device, one `room-<groupId>` channel per group — because that model was already proven with real concurrent devices; only the DOM manipulation became React state. Files: `identity.js` (the device token + local identity store as a module singleton, not React state — it belongs to the browser, not a component's lifecycle), `useLobby.js` (presence, grouping, room-channel lifecycle), `Lobby.jsx` + `GroupEditor.jsx` (presentational), `GameRoom.jsx` (wires a live round to the room channel). `App.jsx` now switches between `<Lobby>` and `<GameRoom>` based on whether a `game-started` broadcast has landed — the first real (if minimal) instance of the round having an owner, ahead of Stage D's full state machine.

**This is also where the "a player taps, the guide cannot" decision below got its first real implementation**, not just a design. `useLobby`'s room channel is created with `broadcast: { self: true }`, and exposes `sendForkChoice`/`onForkChoiceReceived` rather than ever calling `applyChoice` from a local tap — every device, sender included, only acts on the copy the relay sends back. Both `fork-choice` and `game-started` handlers are registered *before* `.subscribe()` (not after — adding `.on()` post-subscribe isn't something supabase-js documents as supported).

**Verified with two real, independently-identified browser sessions against the live Supabase project** (not a mock, not a single-device simulation): "Kahu" and "Aroha" joined the real `lobby-room` presence channel under distinct tokens, were grouped into `Group 5` via the broadcast-and-apply flow (confirmed independently on both sides), auto-joined `room-5`, and one device's "Start Sky Path" click produced a `game-started` broadcast that assigned **guide to Aroha and player to Kahu on both devices independently** — no shared state, no coordinator, just each client comparing the broadcast `guideToken` to its own token. Kahu's device (player) correctly showed the fork buttons with the hint "you cannot see which way is safe"; Aroha's device (guide) showed the safe/hazard markers with buttons hidden entirely.

**The core mechanic — the relay as arbiter — worked exactly as designed on the first real cross-device test.** Kahu (player) tapped `right` (the correct side, confirmed from the guide's own view first). That tap went out as a `fork-choice` broadcast and came back through Supabase's relay to *both* devices; neither device ever applied the tap locally. Kahu's own device only advanced (`correctCount: 1`, `walking: true`) once the broadcast returned — proving the "not even the sender applies its own tap directly" design actually holds, not just in theory. Aroha's device (guide, which never touched a button) advanced identically from the same relayed message. Both were pumped forward and arrived at fork 2 independently, in lockstep, with matching `forkIndex`, `sunP`, and `correctCount` — this is the anti-divergence property the whole "let the relay be the arbiter" design depends on, now observed rather than assumed. **Still not tested: genuine *simultaneous* taps from two players at once** (this test was one player tapping once) — the TODO's original caveat about needing that specific test stands; what's now confirmed is that the single-tap relay round-trip and role-split work correctly, which the concurrent case builds on.

**A real bug found and fixed by porting to React — stale/duplicate presence entries.** `lobby-room` is a public channel on the real shared Supabase project (already known — see the step-3 note below about "Bob, Jill, Jim" wandering in from other sessions). Supabase presence's `presenceState()` maps a key to an *array* of metas, and that array can transiently hold more than one entry per key during a reconnect (a dropped socket's old presence hasn't timed out when a new one for the same token joins) — the standalone prototype's plain `Object.values(state).flat()` silently rendered duplicate `<li>`s for this with no error, since vanilla DOM doesn't validate list identity. React does, and immediately surfaced it as "Encountered two children with the same key." Fixed by taking the newest meta per presence key, **and** de-duplicating by the payload's own `token` field as a second pass, since a stale session from a different origin/port hitting the same public channel can in principle carry a presence key that doesn't match its own tracked token (observed live: three duplicate "Jill" rows collapsed to one after the fix, using real leftover data from earlier testing today, not synthetic input). This is a genuine reliability fix for the "reconnecting students on school wifi" case the handoff calls out, not just a React-specific workaround.

**Two testing-environment traps hit and resolved, recorded so they aren't re-debugged as app bugs:**
  - **Multiple browser tabs share one origin's localStorage.** Simulating a second "device" by opening a second tab does *not* give it a separate identity token — both tabs' `getOrCreateToken()` read the same stored value until one explicitly clears it (the "New device" button). Reloading a tab that hasn't reset first silently adopts whatever token the *other* simulated device most recently wrote — this cost real debugging time before being recognized (a tab reporting "Guide view" incorrectly on both devices turned out to be exactly this, compounded by an in-flight code edit forcing an unrelated tab's HMR reload at the wrong moment). Only ever compare identities via a value read fresh from the page after the "New device" click, never assume a tab's identity is stable across a reload during multi-tab testing.
  - **A stale UI element reference silently degrades to a non-React-aware DOM write.** Using a `form_input`-style ref obtained *before* a page reload, without re-reading the page after, produced a case where the input's raw DOM `.value` was set correctly but React's controlled `useState` never saw the change — `join('')` fired with an empty name and no error anywhere. The fix is procedural, not a code fix: always re-fetch element references after any reload before driving them.

#### Decision: a player taps, the guide cannot (agreed 2026-08-23)

Supersedes the current prototype, where only the guide's client shows the left/right buttons. The pedagogy in the handoff is that the guide *speaks* the correct minimal pair aloud, face to face, and a **player** acts on what they heard. With the guide tapping, the guide's speech is decorative and the demo doesn't show the game. So: players see and press the buttons; the guide's client shows the safe/hazard markers and no buttons at all — the exact inverse of today.

- **Consequence — simultaneous taps need arbitration.** Several players can tap at once, and the two obvious client-side fixes both fail: acting optimistically on your own tap diverges from what others see, and "first message wins by arrival order" diverges too, because arrival order differs per client.
- **Intended solution — let the relay be the arbiter.** Supabase broadcast is relayed through one server that serialises messages, so *every* subscriber observes the same order. If no client ever acts on its own tap directly, and instead every client (sender included, via `config: { broadcast: { self: true } }`) acts only on messages received back from the channel, accepting only the **first** `fork-choice` seen for a given `forkIndex` and ignoring later ones, then all clients converge with no host, no election and no server code. Costs one round trip of latency on the tap, which is the right trade for a turn-based game.
- **Partially verified — see A3 below.** This design is now actually built (in `app/src/lobby/useLobby.js`, not just sketched) and the *single-tap* round trip was confirmed against the real Supabase project: a player's own device only advanced after its tap came back through the relay, and a second, non-tapping device (the guide) advanced identically from the same broadcast, with both landing on matching state. What that test did **not** exercise is the actual point of contention this design is for — two players tapping at, or near, the same instant. **Still needs a deliberate two-device simultaneous-tap test before this is trusted under real classroom conditions**; the fallback if ordering doesn't hold is to nominate the guide's client as round host (players send `fork-request`, host broadcasts authoritative `fork-resolved`) — more machinery, needs host migration on disconnect, but unambiguous.

#### Decision: consolidate first (agreed 2026-08-23)

Stage A before Stage D, rather than bolting a two-way channel onto the existing separate-page Sky Path to reach a demo sooner. Slowest route to something visible — the extraction of `main.js` has no user-visible payoff at all — but every later stage becomes cheap and nothing built gets thrown away. Chosen explicitly on "done right, not quickly." `prototype-threejs/` and the other prototype folders stay in place as reference until Stage A's acceptance test passes, then get removed.

### Multiplayer: participant identity must be designed roster-ready from the start
- **Status**: All five build-sequence steps now have a working proof — identity, Supabase relay, live participant sync, grouping, the real Sky Path game running networked per room, and now persistent classes with a real teacher account and roster (see below). What's left is turning these five separate prototypes into one real app: a React lobby UI, teacher-role gating, a proper join-by-QR flow, and wiring the roster-backed identity store into the actual multi-device lobby (proven standalone, not yet integrated with lobby-prototype's group/room flow).
- **Why this is here**: the original brief called for points/rewards that persist across game modes within a lesson, and scores/equipment that persist across days so a student can log back in as the same character. That requirement had fallen out of scope while the diorama/graphics work was underway and was restored on 2026-08-23 — see [ESL-classroom-game-HANDOFF.md](ESL-classroom-game-HANDOFF.md) ("Persistent classes, scores & equipment" section) for the full reasoning. Recording it here too so it isn't lost a second time.
- **What this changes**: not the architecture (the two-layer session/game-mode split and "identity keyed by stable token, not connection" rule already anticipated this) — it changes **sequencing**. Specifically:
  - **Score and inventory must live on the participant's identity, not on the room.** A room (a Sky Path run, a quiz round) reports results up to the session layer as "this participant earned N points / item X this round"; the session layer is what credits it to the participant record. No game mode should accumulate or read a running total itself.
  - **Equipment** (cosmetic/functional items a character accumulates, e.g. rewards from Sky Path) is a column on the same roster row as score — same shape, no separate model, but worth a schema slot from day one rather than retrofitting once score is already a bare integer.
  - **The participant identity model (build-sequence step 1, not yet started) must be designed as an opaque stable token whose lookup (token → display name/score/equipment) goes through one layer** — backed by local-only state initially, swapped for a real Supabase roster row once teacher accounts/roster CRUD are built (step 5). The lobby and game-mode code should not need to change when that swap happens.
  - **Whole-class shared-screen pattern** (a prompt shown on the projected main screen while students answer privately on their own phones — e.g. a quiz round) is confirmed as a variant of the existing whole-class mode (lobby without grouping = one room containing everyone), not a new architectural case. The projector client already renders room state; this just means a game mode can also target the projector as a display surface, not only the phones.
- **Not yet started, but not blocking Sky Path polish**: none of this needs building before further single-player Sky Path work continues — it's the shape the *next* phase (lobby + multiplayer relay) needs to be built in from the outset.
- **What's built so far**: [identity/token.js](app/src/identity/token.js) (opaque token generation + localStorage persistence, injectable storage for testing) and [identity/identityStore.js](app/src/identity/identityStore.js) (the `getParticipant`/`setDisplayName`/`setGroup`/`addScore`/`addEquipment` interface, with `createLocalIdentityStore()` as step 1's implementation). The interface is documented in identityStore.js precisely so a `createSupabaseIdentityStore()` can implement it later (step 5) without the lobby or game-mode code changing. [identity/identityStore.test.mjs](app/src/identity/identityStore.test.mjs) is a plain-node contract test (token survives a simulated reload, two tokens don't collide, score accumulates rather than overwrites, duplicate equipment doesn't double up) — run with `node identity/identityStore.test.mjs`.
- **Supabase Realtime relay: proven working.** [relay-smoke-test/index.html](relay-smoke-test/index.html) opens a `smoke-test-room` broadcast channel via `@supabase/supabase-js` (loaded from esm.sh, no build step) and logs every ping it sends or receives. Verified with two independent browser tabs, each with its own randomly generated client ID, each subscribed to the same channel: a ping sent from tab A appeared in tab B's log and vice versa, with no direct connection between the tabs — confirming the relay works exactly as the architecture needs (no server code, no peer-to-peer/WebRTC, pub/sub over a WebSocket channel Supabase hosts). Project credentials live in `relay-smoke-test/config.js` (gitignored — copy `config.example.js` and fill in your own project's URL + publishable/anon key from Project Settings → API). To re-run: serve the folder statically (e.g. `python -m http.server 8123` from inside `relay-smoke-test/`) and open the page in two tabs/devices.
- **Identity + relay wired together: proven working.** [lobby-prototype/index.html](lobby-prototype/index.html) + [lobby.js](lobby-prototype/lobby.js) join a Supabase Presence channel (`lobby-room`) keyed by each device's identity token — the identity module's `getOrCreateToken()` and `createLocalIdentityStore()` from step 1, not new state. Each device `track()`s its own participant record (name, score, equipment) into presence; every device's `presence sync` handler re-renders the full list from `channel.presenceState()`. Verified with two tabs acting as two devices (one reset via the page's "New device" button, since tabs in one browser otherwise share localStorage and thus the same token): both joined under different names and each immediately saw the other in its participant list, then clicking the test "+1 point" button in one tab updated that participant's score live in the *other* tab's list with no refresh or polling — proving the actual data model (participant record, not just a raw message) survives the round trip through Supabase. UI is intentionally bare (a name box, a button, a plain list) — this is a proof of the sync mechanism, to be replaced by the real lobby screen later.
- **Grouping: proven working, both random and manual through one mechanism.** The same [lobby.js](lobby-prototype/lobby.js) now has a "Group management" panel: an editable table (name + group-number input per participant) that either "Randomise" fills in (shuffles current participants into equal-ish chunks of a chosen size) or a person edits by hand — both end up as the same `{token: groupNumber}` shape, matching the handoff's "manual and random produce the same data shape." "Apply groups" broadcasts that shape once (`groups-updated` broadcast event, not presence); every device applies only the row matching its own token — via `identityStore.setGroup()` — and re-tracks its presence entry with the new `groupId`, which is what makes the change visible to everyone (presence sync already re-renders on any change). The participant list now renders grouped ("Group 1: Kahu, Rewi" / "Group 2: Aroha"), reading like the rooms it will become, per the handoff's `group` → `room` model.
  - **Verified with three simulated devices**: one device randomised into groups of 2 and clicked "Apply groups"; the other two devices updated their group membership and their own "Your group" label immediately, with no action needed on their end — proving group assignment survives the relay the same way participant sync did.
  - **Known gaps, deliberately deferred**: no teacher-role gating yet (every device can open Group management — the doc's plan is only a teacher-role client shows this), no manual per-student assignment UI beyond typing into the number inputs (fine for proving the mechanism, not a real teacher UX), and the group-editor table rebuilds from scratch on every presence sync so in-progress edits can be clobbered by a participant joining/leaving mid-edit — acceptable for a proof, not for the real lobby.
- **Full `session → group → room → game-mode` pipe: proven working, with concurrent independent rooms.** [lobby.js](lobby-prototype/lobby.js)'s `ensureRoomChannel()` has each device auto-join a *separate* Supabase channel named `room-<groupId>` the moment its own `groupId` is known (and re-join if the group changes) — a different channel/topic per group, which is what gives two groups' rooms independence with zero server-side logic: a device subscribed to `room-5` structurally cannot receive anything sent on `room-6`. The trivial "game mode" is a text box that broadcasts a message into your own room's channel (`game-message` broadcast event). This is the step-3 deliverable from the build sequence — proving the pipe end to end before any real game mode (Sky Path) is wired to it.
  - **Verified with three simulated devices across two concurrent groups**: DeviceA + DeviceB grouped into Group 5, DeviceC alone in Group 6 (chosen deliberately not to collide with a pre-existing Group 1 from other participants already connected to the same lobby channel). Each device's "Room status" correctly showed `in room-5` or `in room-6` immediately after grouping, with no manual join step. A message sent from DeviceA appeared in DeviceB's log but never in DeviceC's; a message sent from DeviceC (the reverse direction) appeared only in DeviceC's own log — full isolation and concurrency confirmed both ways, with the two rooms running at the same time without interfering.
  - **Note from testing**: the same Supabase project's `lobby-room` channel already had other participants connected (names "Bob", "Jill", "Jim" in "Group 1") from outside this session — a reminder that this is a real shared backend now, not a sandboxed local test; anyone with the project's publishable key and channel name can join it. Not a problem for the architecture (clients are trusted per the handoff's "not a cheat-prevention problem" decision) but worth knowing before assuming a test run is isolated.
- **Sky Path itself, running networked per room: proven working (build-sequence step 4).** This is the real game, not a trivial stand-in — [prototype-threejs/src/multiplayer.js](prototype-threejs/src/multiplayer.js) is a no-op unless the URL carries `?room=<id>&role=guide|player` (main.js's existing `?forks=LLLRRR` override already existed from testing and turned out to be exactly the mechanism needed to keep every device's maze identical). When present, the device joins the *same* `room-<groupId>` channel already proven in the trivial pipe test — literally the same channel, not a new one — and either broadcasts fork choices (guide) or replays them (player).
  - **Guide-only fork decisions.** `choose()` was renamed to a plain named function `applyChoice(side)` so it can be called both from a button click and from a network event. `refreshUI()` now hides the left/right buttons entirely for a networked `role==='player'` — deliberately one-sided for this first wiring: only the guide's client can act on a fork at all, everyone else just watches and replays. The real game may later let any player click once they've heard the guide's spoken cue, matching the handoff's actual pedagogy more closely (the guide says the correct word aloud; a player, not the guide, acts on it) — that needs conflict handling for simultaneous clicks that this prototype deliberately doesn't attempt.
  - **Role display, not toggle, when networked.** The existing "Guide view"/"Player view" toggle button (originally a solo-testing convenience for previewing both perspectives on one device) is disabled and just reflects the assigned role when `?room=` is present, since role is now assigned by the lobby, not locally switchable.
  - **lobby-prototype's "Start Sky Path" button** ([lobby.js](lobby-prototype/lobby.js)) generates the shared `forks` sequence and picks a guide token from the room's own members, broadcasts both as a `game-started` event on the same room channel, and every device (including the sender) computes its own role by comparing its own token to the broadcast guide token — no extra round trip needed, since everyone already has everyone else's token from presence. Each device gets a link into `prototype-threejs/index.html` carrying `?forks=&role=&room=`.
  - **Verified with two real devices in one group, using the actual Sky Path Vite app (not a mock).** DeviceCAroha was picked as guide, Kahu as player, both handed `forks=LLRLLR&room=6`. The guide's screen showed the safe/hazard markers and hint ("you can see which way is safe"); the player's screen showed neither, with the hint correctly reading "you cannot see which way is safe," and no left/right buttons at all. Clicking "Left" (correct) on the guide's client advanced *both* devices from Fork 1 to Fork 2 with no action on the player's device — confirmed by screenshot, the player's scene visually walked forward (sun position, pillar layout all updated) purely from the network event. Then clicking "Right" (wrong) on the guide produced the identical fall outcome on both screens: "The path ran out — you fell at fork 2 of 6, after 1 safe crossing." Both the correct-choice and wrong-choice code paths sync correctly.
  - **Config**: `prototype-threejs/src/multiplayer-config.js` (gitignored, same pattern as the other prototypes) holds the shared project's URL + publishable key. Local testing needs Sky Path served through Vite (`npm run dev`, not a plain static server) since its bare-specifier imports (`three`, `@dimforge/rapier3d-compat`) only resolve through Vite's dev server — lobby.js's `SKY_PATH_BASE_URL` points at that dev server directly for now; a real single-deploy build would use a relative link instead.
  - **Known gaps, deliberately deferred**: "Reset"/"Again" after a fall isn't networked — clicking it only restarts the local device's walker, so a player and guide could drift out of sync if either resets independently (would need a `game-reset` broadcast, same pattern as `fork-choice`, not yet built). No handling for a guide disconnecting mid-game (players would simply stop receiving choices). `SKY_PATH_N_FORKS` in lobby.js is a hand-kept-in-sync duplicate of main.js's `N_FORKS`, acceptable for two independent static prototypes but not for the real build.
- **Not built yet**: no React scaffold, no real join-by-QR flow, no teacher-role distinction, no distinction yet between "device reconnects" and "brand new participant" beyond what the identity token already gives for free, no networked reset/disconnect handling (see above), and no "any player can click after hearing the cue" model — this whole prototype is the lobby/session layer plus a first real game-mode wiring, per the handoff's two-decoupled-layers architecture. Config setup mirrors relay-smoke-test: `lobby-prototype/config.js` and `prototype-threejs/src/multiplayer-config.js` (both gitignored) hold the same project's URL + publishable key.

### Persistent classes: real teacher account, roster and score persistence (build-sequence step 5)
- **Status**: ✓ Proven working end to end against the real Supabase project — see below for what's built and what's still just a standalone proof, not yet wired into the multi-device lobby.
- **Real schema, not a mock**: [persistent-classes/schema.sql](persistent-classes/schema.sql) — `classes` (owned by a teacher's `auth.users` row) and `roster_entries` (`class_id`, `display_name`, `score`, `equipment` jsonb, unique per class+name). RLS policies: teachers fully manage their own classes/roster via `auth.uid() = teacher_id`; the anon/publishable key (what students use, no student account per the handoff) can `select`/`update` any `roster_entries` row but never `insert`/`delete` — so only the teacher can add or remove names, matching "students pick from a pre-existing roster," and matching the handoff's explicit "not a cheat-prevention problem" decision (this is deliberately not scoped further, e.g. by join code — see the SQL file's own comment on that trade-off). This is a one-time manual step: I can't run DDL against the project myself with only the publishable key, so the user ran this file in the Supabase SQL Editor before any of the below could be tested.
- **The roster-backed identity store**: [identity/supabaseIdentityStore.js](app/src/identity/supabaseIdentityStore.js) implements the *exact same* interface documented in [identity/identityStore.js](app/src/identity/identityStore.js) (`getParticipant`/`setDisplayName`→`claimByName`/`setGroup`/`addScore`/`addEquipment`), the thing step 1 was written to make possible — the difference forced by being network-backed rather than local: every method is async, and a token has to be `claimByName()`'d against a roster row before `getParticipant` means anything (a device isn't "someone" in a class until it's picked a name). Group assignment stays in an in-memory `Map`, never written to the roster row — grouping is a lobby-session concept, persistence is a roster concept, and this store keeps that boundary the handoff draws.
- **Teacher UI**: [persistent-classes/teacher.html](persistent-classes/teacher.html) + [teacher.js](persistent-classes/teacher.js) — real Supabase Auth (email+password `signUp`/`signInWithPassword`, session restored on reload via `getSession()`), create a class, add/remove roster names, see live scores. Deliberately plain UI, proving the CRUD loop and RLS, not the final teacher UX.
- **Student join/persistence proof**: [persistent-classes/student.html](persistent-classes/student.html) + [student.js](persistent-classes/student.js) — pick a name from the class's roster (fetched via `listRoster()`), claim it, see score/equipment, `+1 point`. A "Simulate new day" button clears this device's stored identity token (`clearToken()` from the existing step-1 module) to stand in for a different day or a different shared classroom device, then re-picking the same name resumes wherever the score was.
- **Verified against the live project, not a fake store**: signed up a real teacher account (`skypath.testteacher2@gmail.com` — the project has "Confirm email" on by default, which the user turned off for this test project since a prototype account has no real inbox to confirm from; worth turning back on before any real classroom use), created class "Year 5 English," added roster names "Aroha" and "Kahu." Joined as a student, picked "Aroha," clicked +1 point twice (score → 2), clicked "Simulate new day" (device got a brand-new identity token, confirmed different from before), picked "Aroha" again on that fresh "device" — **score came back as 2**, not reset to 0, because it was never on the device to begin with. Reloading the teacher's roster view independently confirmed the same score, proving the write actually landed in Postgres rather than just being cached client-side.
- **Known gaps, deliberately not built yet**: no integration with lobby-prototype's multi-device group/room flow (this is a standalone proof of the identity-store swap, not yet the actual lobby using it — a student currently can't pick a roster name *and* get grouped into a room in the same flow); no fallback path coded for a student whose name isn't on the roster (per the RLS, they simply can't claim anything — the intended fallback is dropping back to the ephemeral `createLocalIdentityStore()`, not yet wired up as an automatic choice); no equipment-earning game logic anywhere yet (the roster column exists and `addEquipment()` works, but nothing in Sky Path calls it); anon key can read/edit *any* class's roster scores project-wide, not just ones the student legitimately joined — acceptable for this prototype's trust model, flagged in schema.sql as worth reconsidering before a multi-school deployment.
- **Files**: [ESL-classroom-game-HANDOFF.md](ESL-classroom-game-HANDOFF.md) — full design rationale and the revised build sequence (steps 0–5).

### Guide camera: confirmed broken with a real phone + browser game, goals recorded before further changes
- **Status**: Confirmed broken live, 2026-09-13 — Luke played a real round across a phone (QR-joined) and a browser tab, same game instance. Investigating; deliberately going slowly rather than shipping another unverified guess — see the four goals below, which are the acceptance bar for calling this done, not a description of a fix already made.
- **Superseded note**: the entry that used to be here (2026-09-12) flagged that everything had only ever been tested via the `window.__testUpdateTeammate` debug hook, never a real second device. It's now been tried with a real second device, and it's broken — so that specific risk (a bug in `useLobby.js`'s real broadcast wiring that debug-hook testing couldn't see) is a live suspect again, not a theoretical one.
- **The four goals, Luke's own words, 2026-09-13** — treat every one of these as required, independently checkable behaviour, not a single vague "guide camera works" bar:
  1. When the last remaining player at a fork chooses the WRONG path (falls), the guide's camera must correctly follow them through the fall. Confirmed broken.
  2. When the last remaining player at a fork chooses the CORRECT path, the guide's camera must correctly follow them to the next island. Also confirmed broken — Luke: "the guide camera is [not] ever working properly even if the last player chooses the right path." This matters: it means the fall case isn't a special-case bug, something more fundamental in the follow mechanism itself is wrong.
  3. While parked and watching, the guide must see the FULL movement of any player still in the game, all the way through — not a cut-short or frozen animation. Luke: "the guide view [isn't] showing the players moving fully."
  4. Once every player has either reached the final island or fallen (nobody left active), the guide's own camera must advance to the correct next island and show that island's correct word.
- **First confirmed bug, fixed and verified 2026-09-13**: `guideLastDepartedToken` (skyPath.js) was a single module-level variable, overwritten on *every* departure from *any* fork, not scoped to "whoever most recently left the fork the guide is currently parked at." Proven live (not guessed): with the guide parked at fork 1 watching P1/P2, a THIRD player already ahead at fork 2 departing on their own was enough to make the guide's fork-1 logic think it should follow that fork-2 player instead — confirmed via `__guideCam()` before the fix, confirmed fixed after. Replaced with `guideLastDepartedTokenByFork` (a Map keyed by fork index), plus an extra guard in `updateGuideCamera()` requiring the tracked entry to still actually be `departing` from that same fork right now (an entry object is reused across forks as the same token progresses, so a stale reference could otherwise point at a token that's since moved on elsewhere). Re-ran the exact adversarial sequence after the fix: the guide correctly ignored the distant fork-2 departure and followed the real last-decider at fork 1. This is a real bug and a real fix, not a hypothesis — but seeing it live via debug-hook simulation doesn't by itself prove it's the *only* thing wrong with what Luke saw; see below.
- **Retested live 2026-09-13 (phone + browser again) after the fix above: partially better, four further issues found.** The park/wait/follow *sequencing* itself now works — the guide correctly waits for the last player and correctly moves on afterward. What's still wrong is what the guide actually SEES while doing it. Recorded here in Luke's own words, as the new acceptance bar, before any of these are touched:
  1. **The guide's camera skips ahead to the next island instead of following the last player's actual journey there.** The guide's view during that crossing should be the same as the journeying player's own view — a real ride-along, not a jump cut.
  2. **The guide is seeing the fog-obscuring stand-in animation** (the generic "step forward and disappear" walk built for hiding a PLAYER's choice from other players/teammates who haven't chosen yet) — that animation must never reach the guide. It exists to conceal a direction from someone who could still be influenced by it; the guide can never choose anything, so there is nothing left to protect once someone starts walking, and the guide is specifically supposed to watch the real thing (this was the original intent behind "the guide will watch until the animation is complete" — the fog stand-in built afterwards for player-to-player concealment was never meant to apply to the guide's own view). Goals 1 and 2 are almost certainly one root cause: the guide's followed-player rendering needs to be the REAL branch walk (true side, true duration, true fall/success outcome), not the same short fake straight-line-into-fog placeholder every other player sees. That requires the actually-chosen `side` to reach the guide's device at all, which today it deliberately never does (see the "fog-obscuring departure" entry above) — extending that exclusion for the guide specifically is new scope, not a rename of an existing field.
     - **First pass, ✓ fixed and verified 2026-09-13, goals 1 and 2 together — later superseded, see below.** `applyChoice()` included the real `side` in the `departing` broadcast, and `updateTeammates()` walked the real `sections[k].branch[side]` polyline at a constant `WALK_SPEED` for a spectator viewer — a *simulated* real path, not the actual thing, but a real improvement over the fog stand-in it replaced (for a spectator only; a player's own view was and remains completely unaffected).
     - **Superseded the same day.** Luke, having seen the simulated version live: "the guide is still seeing a stand-in in place of the player's actual movement... it would be good if we could see the players progressing rather than using a stand-in" — explicitly fine with "a short delay and/or a minor displacement" in exchange. So the simulated walk was replaced with genuine live tracking: **✓ fixed and verified 2026-09-13.** While a leg is in progress, the walking device now periodically broadcasts its own real position (`notifyPlayerState('moving', false, {x, z, heading})`, throttled to one ping per `MOVING_PING_INTERVAL` = 150ms — see tick()'s walk block) — safe for the same reason `willFall`/`side` already were, since a player's own view never reads a 'moving' payload at all (see updateTeammates' role check), only a spectator can. `updateTeammate()` applies a 'moving' ping to `entry.livePos`/`entry.livePosAt` only when the entry is still actually departing from the same fork the ping claims (guards a reused entry object against a stale/out-of-order message). `updateTeammates()`, for a spectator role, now smooths (`Vector3.lerp`, frame-rate-independent via `dt`) toward `entry.livePos` whenever it's fresher than `LIVE_POS_STALE_MS` (500ms — comfortably more than one ping interval, so ordinary jitter is never mistaken for a stop). There is no explicit "I've started falling" signal on the wire — a real fall is client-local physics, and the pings (only sent while `leg` is active) simply stop the instant it begins — so once a `willFall` entry's pings go stale, it sinks/tips from wherever it was last actually seen for a fixed `FALL_STANDIN_DURATION` (0.6s, still a simplified cosmetic cue, not real fall physics — see this section's own comment on why that stays a separate undertaking) then vanishes; if pings go stale and `willFall` is false, the real arrival is imminent, so the last known spot is simply held until the real 'resting' broadcast (from that same device, once it actually gets there) takes over exactly as it always has. The now-unnecessary simulated-path machinery (`pointAlongPath`, the per-frame `pathLength` recompute, the `side` field itself) was removed rather than left dead — nothing reads `side` any more, since live tracking supersedes what it existed for.
     - **Testing note for future work on this system**: driving this test with real-time `await new Promise(r => setTimeout(...))` gaps produced misleadingly frozen positions at first — this Browser-pane tab, not being genuinely focused, throttles `requestAnimationFrame` to ~1fps, so `tick()` (and therefore the position lerp) barely ran during the wait. Switched to manually calling `window.__tick()` in a tight loop (same fix TODO.md's falling-mechanic entry already documented for exactly this environment quirk) to actually advance frames, keeping real `setTimeout` gaps only where the test specifically needed genuine wall-clock time to pass (to cross `LIVE_POS_STALE_MS`). Verified three scenarios this way: fresh pings smoothly tracked toward each waypoint; a `willFall` entry correctly sank/tipped from its last live position once pings went stale, then vanished; a successful entry correctly held its last position (no jump, no drift) through a stale gap until a real 'resting' update cleanly repositioned it at the next fork. A normal player view was re-confirmed unaffected (still exactly the fog stand-in) even while `livePos` was actively being sent. **Still not verified with a real second device** — same standing caveat as everywhere else in this file, and the one this specific feature (network chatter timing, real device pacing) would benefit from most.
  3. **A player who has fallen (Watch mode) is not getting the guide's kind of view at all** — instead their camera appears to snap back to the FIRST island (now empty), not to wherever the group actually is. A fallen player's Watch mode should behave like the guide: advance island to island, watching whoever's currently moving, until the round ends. **The one deliberate difference from the guide's view**: a fallen player already had their own fixed per-round seating arrangement (see the "fixed per-round seating chart" entry above — self always centre, everyone else in a fixed left-right order) from when they were still playing; Watch mode should keep using THAT fixed arrangement (so teammates appear in the same relative order the fallen player always saw them in), not whatever arrangement the guide happens to use.
     - **First attempt (guideIsland seeding): worked in isolation, missed the actual architecture problem.** Diagnosed the "always starts at fork 1" symptom as Watch mode mounting a brand-new SkyPath instance with no memory of round progress, and fixed exactly that (threading `round.result.forkIndex` in as an `initialGuideIsland` seed) — this part was correct and is still in place as the cold-mount path (see below), verified via `?solo=1&role=watching&guideIsland=N` and confirmed again with a full real two-device test (real teacher session, real character selection, real held walk across a real bridge, a real fall at fork 2 — landed correctly on `guideIsland: 2`, not 1).
     - **But Luke reported it was still completely broken, because the actual live symptom was never about `guideIsland` at all.** His report: "the only thing that has changed is that the 'Again' button has been removed. Otherwise things happen exactly the same way," plus the key diagnostic clue — "a loading screen after the player falls suggests a restart of some sort. In no way should the game be restarting... after the player falls, what happens next should be something new, not a return to something else." That was exactly right, and something a full re-test of the already-fixed piece would never have caught, because that piece really was fixed — the bug was elsewhere: **every fall was tearing down the entire THREE.js scene and rebuilding it from scratch**, because routing a failed round to a brand-new `<GameRoom round={{...lobby.round, role:'watching'}}>` forces the `role` PROP to change on what React (and `SkyPath.jsx`'s own mount effect, which lists `role` as a dependency) still often treats as a comparable element — and even where it doesn't remount the React component itself, `SkyPath.jsx`'s effect explicitly disposes and rebuilds the whole `mountSkyPath()` instance whenever `role` changes, by design ("the effect re-runs only on the things that genuinely define a different game: the fork sequence, the role..."). That's a REAL loading screen (a fresh `manager.onLoad`), not a UI illusion — and it explains everything Luke saw: the brief "Again" flash (from the dying player instance), the loading screen (the actual rebuild), and landing back at fork 1 with no one visible (a fresh `teammates` map starting empty, `guideIsland` reset before `initialGuideIsland` could apply cleanly against a half-torn-down scene).
     - **✓ Properly fixed and verified 2026-09-13.** Rebuilt so a fall never remounts anything: `skyPath.js` gained `becomeSpectator()` — a method on the running instance's own handle that flips `role`/`canAct` to spectator in place (hides the figure, drops the name tag, seeds `guideIsland` from the closure's OWN current `forkIndex` — no external value needs threading in at all, since the same live instance already knows exactly where it is), with no teardown, no `manager.onLoad`, no fresh `teammates` map. `GameRoom.jsx` now takes a `failed` prop and calls `gameRef.current.becomeSpectator()` from an effect instead of relying on a prop-driven remount. `App.jsx` no longer renders a second, separate `<GameRoom role="watching">` — the SAME `<GameRoom round={lobby.round} failed={...}>` renders straight through 'playing' into a failed 'results' phase, and `round.role` itself never changes to 'watching', so nothing about the element or its props gives React (or SkyPath.jsx's effect) a reason to tear anything down. A genuinely NEW round still remounts correctly and intentionally, since `forks`/`words` genuinely do change then. **Verified live, real two-device test**: attached a `MutationObserver` to the loading overlay's own class list before triggering a real fall, so the check doesn't depend on catching a fast visual flash — `loaderEvents` came back `[]` (the loader's class never changed, meaning `manager.onLoad`'s fresh-load path never ran a second time), while `__guideCam()` correctly showed `role: "watching"` at the right fork, and the on-screen role badge updated to "Watching" with the original "loaded 733 ms" timestamp from the very first mount still showing (proof it's the same scene, not a new one). The old `initialGuideIsland`/`?guideIsland=` mechanism was kept, not removed — it's still the right answer for the rarer cold-mount case (e.g. a page reload while already in Watch mode), just no longer the path a normal in-session fall takes.
     - **Seating-order half is still NOT done** — unchanged from before, still blocked on the separate "fixed per-round seating chart" redesign (see that entry above), which hasn't been built yet at all; Watch mode currently shares the guide's same live, self-less, token-sorted layout, not "the order this player had while they were still playing." Not a regression — there was no seating order to preserve before today's fix either — just flagging that this specific nuance from Luke's original ask isn't covered until that bigger piece is built.
  4. **The guide's avatar is not meant to exist as a physical presence in the game at all, but currently sometimes is.** Concretely: with one guide and three players, every device (the guide's own, and all three players') should behave exactly as if there were only three players in the game — nobody should ever see a fourth figure, name tag, or island occupant for the guide, on any screen. **However — explicitly keep the guide's character-select choice itself, just make it invisible for now**: Luke wants the guide to actually go through the same "choose your character/colour" screen as everyone else (right now it's silently skipped and defaulted, i.e. "the guide is having their avatar chosen for them," not choosing it) — because the *next* stage of the game will rotate who guides between rounds, and whoever guides will need a real stored choice to fall back to as a player. So: keep the character-select screen for the guide, keep storing whatever they pick, just never broadcast or render it while they're actually guiding. **Do not remove the guide's character-select flow or its stored choice as part of fixing this** — that would silently undo groundwork for guide rotation, for a reason that wouldn't be obvious from the code alone months from now. This note exists specifically so that mistake doesn't happen.
     - **✓ Fixed and verified 2026-09-13.** Root cause was `finishCharacterSelect()` calling `notifyPlayerState()` unconditionally, including for spectator roles — the guide's device broadcast itself into every player's `teammates` map exactly like a real teammate, and (since the guide's own `forkIndex` never advances) would have sat there forever as a phantom occupant of fork 1 on every player's screen. Fix, in skyPath.js: `manager.onLoad` now only auto-skips the character-select screen for `role === 'watching'` (a fallen player who already picked once this round) — the guide sees the exact same screen a normal player does, and its pick is stored exactly as before. `finishCharacterSelect()` now skips both `attachNameTag()` and `notifyPlayerState()` for any `isSpectatorRole(role)` (guide or watching) — the rig is already permanently invisible (`figure.visible` in `setCharacter`) so a tag could never be seen anyway, and skipping `notifyPlayerState()` is the actual fix: nothing is ever broadcast for a spectator to appear in anyone else's `teammates` map. Verified live: guide now sees and completes the real character-select screen (confirmed via `__rig()` that the chosen character is actually stored), and its own figure stays invisible afterward with no console errors on a fresh tab. **Not yet verified that a real PLAYER's screen no longer shows a guide entry** — that needs a real second device/session, the same gap flagged elsewhere in this file; the fix is a straightforward one-line guard, but hasn't been watched happen live from a player's point of view.
     - **Follow-up, ✓ fixed and verified 2026-09-13**: with the guide entry itself removed, Luke noticed the guide's OWN view still left an empty gap dead centre — "where their own avatar would have gone." Cause: `layoutIslandGroup`'s ranking always reserved offsetX 0 for a `self` slot regardless of whether a self actually existed to stand there — invisible for a real player (their own body already genuinely occupies 0 in world space) but a real void for the guide (nobody does). Fixed by shifting the OTHERS' rank index by one only when a self is present, so the first other fills the centre slot when there isn't one; verified live both ways — guide watching 3 simulated teammates now shows one centred at x=0 with the rest at ±1.35 (no gap), and a normal player watching 2 teammates is unchanged (±1.35, true centre still reserved for their own real body).
- **Sequencing, per Luke, 2026-09-13**: fix these one at a time and verify each before moving to the next, not as one combined change. Goal 4 done above; goals 1/2 (one root cause) are next, then goal 3.

### Fog-obscuring departure: reported broken live — reveals the direction before hiding it
- **Status**: Reported broken, root cause not yet found. Luke, 2026-09-12: "the player only disappears after they have clearly started to walk along one of the two bridges. It doesn't obscure their choice." On inspection the code (`updateTeammates()`'s `departing` branch in skyPath.js) *looks* correct on paper — it advances the departing rig straight along `sec.fork.heading` (the fork's own centred entry heading, not either branch's angled heading) toward the same centred point the fog curtain itself stands at (`CURTAIN_DIST` past the fork, see `makeCurtain`'s call site), so mathematically nothing here should reveal a side. That mismatch between "looks correct in the source" and "wrong in a real playtest" is itself informative: it points at either (a) the sending device not actually calling `notifyPlayerState('departing', …)` at the right moment (or at all) in the real `applyChoice()` path, so what Luke saw was something else entirely rather than this code running with a bug in it, or (b) this code never having run in a real two-device test before now (see the entry above — same unverified-plumbing risk). Needs a real live trace (two devices, or the debug hook driving the *actual* `applyChoice()` flow rather than calling `updateTeammate()` directly) before guessing further.
- **The agreed fix, once the cause is found**: stop trying to reuse/repair the geometric "walk toward the real curtain position" approach at all. Replace it with a new, independent, short (~1–1.5s, a fixed constant, deliberately *not* derived from `CURTAIN_DIST`/`WALK_SPEED` the way the real walk is) placeholder animation: straight forward into a fog wall positioned directly in front of the departing player, then gone. Purely symbolic, not trying to be physically consistent with the real walk speed.
- **Scope of who sees the placeholder vs. the real thing (this is where the next entry's new idea plugs in)**: the departing player's own device never uses this at all — it always shows its own true, accurate movement, unaffected by anything here. Every other viewer's default is the placeholder. The guide (and a fallen player in Watch mode, which deliberately rides the guide's own camera/state) always gets the placeholder, forever, for anyone — the guide is never supposed to learn which side is correct, no exception.

### Agreed design, not yet built: fixed per-round seating chart, replacing live per-frame relayout entirely
- **Status**: Design agreed 2026-09-12, superseding the "pre-calculated inbound layout" idea directly above (kept struck through, not deleted, since the reasoning about *why* teammates need to become visible once you're past your own fog still holds — only the layout mechanics change). **Supersedes/resolves two specific risks flagged in that entry**: the "provisional/inbound occupant" bucket and its retraction-on-failure case are no longer needed at all — see below.
- **The core change**: today's layout (`layoutIslandGroup`/`repositionIslandOccupants`, called every frame from `relayoutAllIslands()`) recomputes who stands where from scratch, every frame, based on whoever *currently* happens to be resting at an island. Luke's simplification: compute each player's own seating assignment **once**, at round start, from the full group roster — self always centre, everyone else sorted (same deterministic token-sort already used) and assigned alternating sides/ranks exactly as today's algorithm does, but **only once**, then reused unchanged, at every island, for the rest of the round. A teammate's rank and side relative to *me* never changes once assigned, regardless of who has arrived, fallen, or not yet reached that island.
- **No compaction, ever — gaps are permanent and meaningful.** Worked example from Luke: player C (self, always centre) has A and B fixed to their left, D and E fixed to their right. Arriving at an island last, C sees A, B, D, E already standing in exactly those fixed spots, with only the centre gap (themselves) to fill. If D has already fallen by then, C sees *two* gaps: the centre (not yet filled by C) and D's fixed slot on the right (between the empty centre and E) — permanently empty for the rest of the round, not closed up. Carried to its conclusion: by the final island, only one teammate might remain, and they keep whatever fixed rank they were always assigned — which could easily be the outermost ("far left" or "far right") slot, since nothing here ever re-centres around whoever's actually present.
- **Why this is a strict simplification, not just a different feature**: no per-frame recomputation, no reshuffle/pop risk when someone arrives (nothing to reshuffle — the slot was always theirs), and it directly resolves the two open risks in the entry above: an inbound-but-not-yet-arrived teammate has nothing "provisional" to reserve (their slot was fixed from round start regardless of presence), and a teammate who falls before arriving simply never fills a slot that was never going to move anyway — no retraction logic needed. This is very likely also a meaningful simplification toward fixing the still-unverified guide/teammate-visibility system above: a static per-round lookup is far less likely to have the kind of subtle live-state bug a frame-by-frame Map-iteration-order system invites.
- **Watch mode redefined, dropping the borrowed-guide-camera design**: currently a fallen player's "Watch" button reuses the guide's own camera/role wholesale (`role: 'watching'` in App.jsx, riding `isSpectatorRole`'s exact code path). Luke's new instruction: **it should no longer be the guide's view at all.** A fallen player already has their own fixed personal seating chart (they were a full participant right up until they fell) — Watch mode should keep using exactly that, just with their own centre slot now permanently empty (they fell, so there's nothing to draw there — consistent with the no-compaction rule above, not a special case), and their camera switching from player-controlled to automatic, following whichever teammate is currently the last to move — the same *behaviour* the guide's camera already has, but driven through the fallen player's own existing per-round layout rather than a shared guide state machine. **The true guide is a separate case and is not stated to change** — the guide has no personal slot of its own (never a team member), so it's assumed to keep its current camera/arrangement unless Luke says otherwise.
- **Open question to confirm before implementing** (flagging rather than guessing): should the guide *also* move onto some version of this fixed-layout model for consistency — e.g. the full team's fixed order with no self/centre reserved, so a lone survivor shows up at their fixed rank for the guide too, the same way it now would for a still-alive teammate? Not explicitly requested; the message text reads as Watch mode changing and the guide staying as-is. Worth a one-line confirmation before touching the guide's code, since unifying it would remove a second, still-separate camera/layout implementation, but that's a judgement call, not what was asked for.
- **What actually needs to change to build this**:
  - Compute the per-round seating assignment once, keyed by the local player's own token, when the round's group roster is known (the `game-started` broadcast already carries the group — this is the natural trigger), and cache it for the round's duration instead of calling `layoutIslandGroup` from `relayoutAllIslands()` every frame.
  - `repositionIslandOccupants` collapses to: for each teammate in *my* fixed assignment, if they're currently resting at this fork, position them at their fixed rank/side offset; if not (departed, not yet arrived, or fallen), draw nothing. No sorting-by-who's-currently-present at all any more.
  - Watch mode (`App.jsx`'s results-screen branch, currently forcing `role: 'watching'` into the guide's exact camera code) needs its own camera-follow behaviour built on the *player's* own state instead of reusing `isSpectatorRole`/`guideMode` wholesale — a real change to how Watch mode is wired, not just a rename.
  - The "reveal a teammate's real position once you've personally committed at that fork" idea from the entry above is unaffected in principle and still needed on top of this — but the destination slot for an in-transit teammate is now simply their pre-known fixed offset, which removes the hardest part of that entry's design (see "strict simplification" above).
- **Sequencing, per Luke's explicit instruction**: document this thoroughly (done, this entry) and hold it for implementation soon — **not built yet**. The fog-departure bug above is being fixed first.
- **Superseded 2026-09-13 — see the next entry.** When it actually came time to build this, Luke reconsidered the "self always centre" premise itself, not just the layout mechanics: "I wonder if a lot of it comes down to my insistence that different players in fact see different things... If instead of this each player saw themselves in one position, i.e. player A sees themselves on the left, B to the right of A, C in the middle... would this allow us to... simplify and reduce errors?" The design actually built is a single shared seat table (one fixed offset per token, identical on every device) rather than a per-viewer "self at centre, others relative to me" table. That also **retires the open question right above** about whether the guide should move onto the fixed-layout model too — under the single-table design there's only ever one table, and the guide reads the exact same one everyone else does (it just never gets a slot in it, having no seat of its own), so there's no separate "guide's own arrangement" left to decide about.

### ✓ Built 2026-09-13: single shared seat table + diagonal seat-walk (fixed seating, take two)
- **What changed from the superseded plan above**: not "self always centre, others fixed relative to me" — a single, round-wide table (`seatOffsets` in skyPath.js), built once from `roster` — the group's player tokens in one fixed order, broadcast by the teacher in the same `game-started` payload as `forks`/`words` (see `TeacherDashboard.jsx`'s `startGame()`) — assigning each token a lateral offset by its plain array index (same alternating-sides-outward-from-centre pattern the old per-frame ranking used). Every device in the group receives the identical array and so builds the identical table with no further messages — a real single shared fact, not several devices' independent opinions about the same layout. Self is just another entry in this same table now (looked up via a new `myToken` option), not a permanently-reserved centre slot — which is also what retires the old guide-only "empty centre" special case from goal 4 above, structurally rather than by another one-off fix.
- **The diagonal walk**, per Luke: "you will have to draw the player moving to their correct position... players will need to walk at an angle (towards the centre when leaving an island, away from the centre when leaving the bridge to find their position on the next island)." `currentSeatLateral()` computes this every frame as a pure function of the in-progress `leg`: the seat offset is full while resting, tapers linearly down to zero over the first `SEAT_TAPER_DIST` (= `ISLAND_RADIUS`) of a leg's travel, and — only if the leg actually has a next island (`leg.arriveFork`) — back up to that same fixed offset over the last `SEAT_TAPER_DIST`. A leg with no `arriveFork` (a wrong choice, or the final approach to the temple) only ever tapers OUT and rides the centreline the rest of the way — deliberately, since both of those endings are scripted, centreline-only sequences (the fall, the temple doors) that the fall physics/rescue cinematic/curtain math is all built assuming, not a seat to arrive at. The underlying `walker.x/z` simulation (bridge height, fall physics, sun timing) is completely untouched by any of this — the seat walk is a render-time lateral offset applied on top, at every place the rendered position matters: the local figure, the trailing camera (position AND look-at, so the player still feels centred in their own view even while visibly offset in world space to everyone else), the key light target, and — critically — the position broadcast in the periodic 'moving' ping, so a spectator (guide or a fallen player's Watch mode) sees the real diagonal walk with no extra work on the receiving end, exactly the same live-tracking path goal 2 above already built.
- **Resting placement** (`repositionIslandOccupants`/`relayoutAllIslands`) collapsed the same way the superseded plan predicted: no more per-frame sort-by-token/rank-from-scratch, no more `includeSelf` branching — every teammate is simply placed at ITS OWN fixed `entry.seatOffsetX` (set once, in `ensureTeammateEntry`, from the shared table) whenever it's resting; self's own resting placement isn't handled by this function at all any more, since `currentSeatLateral()`'s `!leg` branch already places self the same way, every frame, whether resting or walking.
- **Found and fixed along the way, not the seating work itself**: while tracing the movement code, found why "the guide and dead player have their view broken when the watched player uses a jetpack to save themselves... no player visible" (reported live in the same message as the seating request). A jetpack rescue mid-round (`resolveRescue()`'s non-last-fork branch) teleports straight to the new fork and was the ONE arrival path in the whole file that never called `notifyPlayerState()` — every other arrival (a normal walk completing, per `leg.arriveFork` in tick()) does. So nobody watching ever learned the rescued player had arrived anywhere; their teammate entry just sat wherever the pre-rescue departure/fall-tracking had last left it. Fixed by adding the same `notifyPlayerState()` call that branch. **Known remaining rough edge, not fixed**: because a rescue is instant with no `leg` and thus no 'moving' pings, and the player's teammate entry was already marked `willFall` at their original wrong-choice departure, a spectator can still briefly see the fall stand-in (sink-and-tip) play out during the rescue cutscene before the new fix's 'resting' report corrects it — cosmetically wrong (a rescue looks like a flash of "falling" first) but no longer leaves anyone invisible.
